What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um, I guess I came in because... well, it's my face. The left side of my face just looks completely off. Like my nose and my jaw are totally crooked and I just can't stop thinking about it. It's so obvious in every ID photo or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. Actually, my student ID picture was a total disaster because the lady taking it was using this really old camera, and the lighting in the student center is just awful. They have those cheap fluorescent bulbs that make this humming sound all day, and I don't know how anyone works in there. It drives me crazy. \n\nBut anyway, even when I point out my face to people, they swear they don't see it. Which is ridiculous. I know everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it. I'm a programmer there, and I feel like they're all just staring at my messed-up jaw before they even notice my work. Which sucks because I do really good work. We just got this new 5-axis milling machine in the shop, and it's incredibly precise, like down to the micron. I spent all of Tuesday writing the G-code for this custom aluminum bracket, and the tool paths were absolutely perfect, but... yeah, I just feel like nobody cares about the parts I make when my face looks like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face might actually look normal to other people?\n\nPatient: I mean... no? How could it? It's right there. I know they say they don't see it, but people just lie to be nice, right? Like my roommate, Dave. He's always saying stuff just to smooth things over. He does that with the apartment, too. Like, he'll swear he cleaned the kitchen, but all he did was wipe the counter with a dry paper towel. He leaves crumbs literally everywhere. We actually had this huge ant problem last month because he left a box of donuts open by the sink for three days. \n\nThe exterminator had to come out, and he was this guy who just wouldn't stop talking about his vintage truck collection. He had like, three old Chevys he was restoring in his garage. I guess restoring old machines is cool, kind of like what we do with the lathes in the shop, but he was telling me about his engine rebuilds and he was using these really outdated welding techniques that just aren't structurally sound long-term... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, if people think my face looks normal. Yeah, no. There's no way they don't see how crooked my jaw is. They're definitely just lying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your jaw actually is straight, but you just perceive it as crooked?\n\nPatient: I... no, that doesn't make any sense. I mean, I look in the mirror every single morning. I can literally trace the line of my jaw with my finger and feel how it dips on the left side. It's not just in my head, it's physical geometry. Like, if I were measuring it with a dial indicator on the mill, the runout would be massive. You know what a dial indicator is, right? We use them to check if a part is perfectly round or if a vise is trammed in straight. I was actually showing the new guy, Kevin, how to tram the vise on the Haas yesterday. Kevin is... well, he's trying, but he keeps zeroing out the machine before he even checks the tool offsets, which is just asking for a crash. I told him, if you crash that spindle, it's like a ten thousand dollar repair, plus the downtime. And the shop manager, Mr. Henderson, he's already stressed because our main supplier for titanium stock is backordered until November. Apparently there's some issue with the shipping ports on the West Coast, like a strike or something, and all the containers are just sitting there rusting. \n\nBut yeah, my jaw. It's definitely not straight. I'm not imagining it. It's just physically wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your perception of your jaw being crooked might be different from reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... reality? What even is reality when I can see it right there in the mirror? I don't think my perception is off. It's just a fact. It's like... it's like when you're looking at a blueprint. The dimensions are either right or they're wrong. And my face is wrong. Speaking of blueprints, we got this crazy complicated schematic in the shop last week from an aerospace contractor. It was for this tiny turbine blade, and the tolerances were insane. Like, plus or minus two ten-thousandths of an inch. I had to spend three hours just calibrating the coolant flow because if the titanium gets even a little bit too hot, it warps, and then the whole part is scrap. And titanium is so expensive right now. We had to lock the stock room because someone from the night shift was supposedly taking scraps home to make custom rings or something. Which is so dumb, because you can't just machine titanium on a regular lathe in your garage without ruining your tools... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about my perception. Yeah, no, I'm not making it up. My jaw is definitely crooked. It's not a perception thing, it's just how it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone could convince you that your jaw is actually straight?\n\nPatient: Convince me? I mean... I don't see how they could. It's like trying to argue with gravity, you know? You can't just talk someone out of a physical fact. It actually reminds me of trying to convince Mr. Henderson to update our CAM software at the shop. We're still running this ancient version of Mastercam from like 2018, and it's constantly glitching out when I try to generate toolpaths for anything with complex 3D surfacing. I literally showed him the error logs on my screen, but he just doesn't want to spend the money on the license renewal. He's so cheap. He even makes us reuse the coolant filters until they're completely black and smelling like rotten eggs. It's disgusting. I told him it's a health hazard, especially since the shop ventilation is basically just a rusty box fan propped up by the loading dock doors. And when it rains, the water pools right near the high-voltage panel for the main air compressor, which has to be a massive OSHA violation. I keep telling Kevin not to step in the puddles wearing his regular sneakers, but he never listens... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, convincing me. Yeah, no. Unless someone can somehow magically change what I'm looking at in the mirror, no one is going to convince me my jaw is straight. It's just not.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your jaw being crooked ever change, or does it always seem the same to you?\n\nPatient: It... no, it doesn't change. It's always there. Every time I look, it's the exact same crooked angle. It's like a constant, you know? Like when you set a zero point on the CNC machine. Once you set that G54 coordinate, it doesn't just magically move unless you tell it to. Which, actually, happened last week when Kevin was running the Haas. He somehow managed to overwrite the work offsets in the middle of a cycle. I still don't even know how he did it, because the control panel locks you out of those settings while the spindle is turning. But suddenly the end mill plunged like three inches too deep into this block of aluminum and just shattered. Sounded like a gunshot. Shrapnel went everywhere, and one piece actually dented the polycarbonate window on the door. Mr. Henderson was furious, obviously, because that was a specialized carbide roughing tool that costs like two hundred bucks. And we had to spend the rest of the afternoon digging aluminum chips out of the coolant tank because the crash broke the chip auger, too. I swear, sometimes I think I'm the only one in that shop who actually checks their code before hitting cycle start...\n\nBut yeah, my jaw. It's always crooked. It never looks straight to me, ever.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that your belief about your jaw being crooked might be false?\n\nPatient: False? No, how could it be false? I mean, I know people will sometimes say they don't see it, but I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. My nose, my jaw... they're just crooked in literally every ID photo I take, and every single time I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. \n\nActually, those online classes are completely ridiculous right now. I'm taking this advanced blueprint reading and metallurgy course at the community college, and the professor insists on using this invasive proctoring software for the quizzes that completely locks down your computer. It flags you for cheating if you even look away from the screen for more than three seconds. Which is literally impossible because I have to look down at my physical reference tables to do the calculations! I got flagged twice last week just for checking the thread pitch on a metric bolt chart. I had to email the department head about it because the professor wouldn't listen. It's like, I'm a CNC programmer, I need to look at my reference charts. If I don't double-check my feeds and speeds, I could snap a tool, you know? Just like I was saying earlier about Kevin crashing the machine. It's just common sense to check your work...\n\nWait, sorry, what was your question? Oh, right, if I think my belief is false. No, I don't. Because even if people say they don't see it to be polite, I know they do. Whenever I go into work, I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face and staring at my jaw before they even notice the parts I'm machining. So no, it's definitely not false.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw being crooked affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. I mean, it affects everything. I've actually missed a bunch of my CNC labs and even some shifts at the shop because I just... I couldn't get myself out the door. I'd stand there looking in the mirror and just couldn't face anyone. My faculty mentor actually pulled me aside and said my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing hours. Which is incredibly stressful because getting that placement was a nightmare in the first place. The coordinator, Mrs. Gable, lost my paperwork twice. Literally twice. I had to go down to the administration building, which is all the way on the other side of campus, past the old gymnasium that they're tearing down. They've had this massive wrecking ball out there for weeks, just smashing into the brickwork. It's so loud you can hear it from the library. And the dust! My car was completely covered in this fine red brick dust for days. I had to take it to the wash three times, and the guy at the wash station tried to upsell me on this ceramic coating thing for like eighty bucks. I told him no, obviously, because I'm a student and I barely have enough money for gas right now, especially since Mr. Henderson keeps cutting my hours at the shop because of my attendance...\n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, relationships and stuff. Yeah, I stopped playing soccer with my friends on the weekends, too. And I used to volunteer at the animal shelter, but I quit that as well. I just don't want people looking at me. I know they're just staring at my jaw the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility, no matter how small, that your jaw might actually be straight, but you're just misinterpreting what you see?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting? No, I... I don't see how that's possible. I'm not just imagining it. It's not a trick of the light or something. It's like... well, in machining, if you think a part looks off, you put it on the CMM—the Coordinate Measuring Machine—and it tells you exactly what the dimensions are down to the ten-thousandth of an inch. You can't really misinterpret a ruby-tipped probe physically mapping the surface of a part. \n\nThough, actually, last month our CMM was giving us these completely wild readings on a batch of titanium aerospace brackets. We thought the parts were warped, but it turned out the calibration sphere was loose because someone—probably Kevin again—bumped it and didn't tell anyone. We had to call the Renishaw tech out to recalibrate the whole probe head, which took like four hours. And the tech, this guy with a really intense mustache, kept talking about his custom mechanical keyboard build the entire time. Like, he actually brought his own keyboard in a special carrying case just to type his diagnostic reports on our computer. It had these incredibly loud clicky switches, Cherry MX Blues or something, and it was just echoing through the whole inspection room. It was driving me insane because I was trying to write a complex toolpath for a 5-axis trunnion setup on my laptop, and all I could hear was clack-clack-clack for half the afternoon. He even tried to convince me to buy a soldering iron to build my own, but I barely have time to sleep right now, let alone solder keyboard switches...\n\nBut, um, anyway. You were asking if I'm just seeing it wrong? No. No, my eyes aren't a faulty probe. I look in the mirror, I look at photos, and it's just... it's completely crooked. There's zero possibility it's actually straight. None.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your concern about your jaw is not based on how it actually looks, but on how you feel about it?\n\nPatient: How I feel about it? No, it's... it's not a feeling. It's a physical reality. I'm not just feeling like it's crooked, it *is* crooked. It's like... it's like when you have a dull endmill. You don't just *feel* like the cut is bad, you can literally see the terrible surface finish and the burrs it leaves behind. \n\nActually, speaking of dull tools, we had this massive order of aluminum heatsinks last week, and the shop manager refused to order new carbide endmills because he said we were over budget for the quarter. So we had to use these old, recut tools that were just completely chewed up. The chatter on the machines was unbelievable. It sounded like a banshee screaming in the shop for eight hours straight. And the coolant... oh man, the coolant pump on machine three has been failing for a month, so instead of a nice steady stream, it just sputters and sprays this nasty, rancid-smelling fluid everywhere. I got it all over my favorite work shirt, the blue one with the company logo on the pocket. I've washed it like four times with heavy-duty detergent and a cup of white vinegar, which my mom swore would get the smell out, but it still smells like old gym socks and machine oil. I ended up just throwing it in the rag bin because I couldn't stand wearing it anymore. It's a shame, too, because it was one of the few shirts that actually fit me right across the shoulders without being too baggy in the waist...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Feelings? No, no, it's not based on my feelings. My jaw is crooked. Anyone with eyes can see it. It's just a fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be overestimating how noticeable your jaw is to other people?\n\nPatient: Overestimating? No, I... I don't think I am. I mean, how could I be? It's right there in the middle of my face. It's like... if you walk into a room and there's a huge, glaring mistake on a blueprint, you can't just ignore it. \n\nLike this one time, we got these prints from an engineering firm in Seattle for some custom brackets, and whoever drafted it completely messed up the GD&T—the Geometric Dimensioning and Tolerancing. They put a true position tolerance on a hole that was tighter than the machine's actual positioning capability. I tried to tell the lead programmer, Dave, but he just brushed me off because he was stressed about his daughter's wedding. Apparently, they were having it at this really expensive vineyard out in wine country, and the caterer canceled on them like three weeks before the date. Dave was on the phone constantly, trying to find a replacement who could do a gluten-free, vegan menu for two hundred people. He ended up finding this food truck company that agreed to do it, but they charged him double their normal rate as a rush fee. He was so distracted he just told me to run the program as-is. Of course, we scrapped the first five parts. The quality control guy, Frank, was furious. Frank is this older guy who used to work in aerospace back in the eighties, and he takes everything so seriously. He literally threw his calipers across the inspection room when he saw the parts. They hit the wall and left this big dent right next to the safety poster...\n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh, right. Noticeable. No, I'm not overestimating it. People definitely notice it. I see them looking at it when they talk to me. They might try to pretend they aren't, but they are. It's impossible not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever be able to accept that your jaw is straight, even if multiple people, including doctors and experts, told you it was?\n\nPatient: Accept it's straight? No. Because they'd be wrong. I mean, even experts get things wrong all the time. Just because someone has a title doesn't mean they actually see what's right in front of them. \n\nLike, we had this \"expert\" technician come in to rebuild the spindle on our Haas VF-4 mill last month. He was supposed to be the top guy in the state, right? He finished the job and told us the runout was practically zero, absolutely perfect. But he was using this cheap dial indicator he probably bought off Amazon. And honestly, he wasn't even paying attention because he kept talking about his dog, this massive Goldendoodle that apparently eats the drywall in his house. He stopped what he was doing to show me like twenty pictures on his phone while he was supposed to be torquing the drawbar. The dog had literally chewed a hole straight through to the insulation in his living room, and he thought it was hilarious. He said he had to patch it with this special wire mesh, but the dog just ate the mesh too, which honestly sounds like a massive vet bill waiting to happen. Anyway, we fired the machine up the next day to run some 316 stainless steel, and the tool chatter was insane. We checked it ourselves with a proper Mitutoyo test indicator, and the runout was like three thousandths! The so-called expert completely botched the bearing preload and just didn't want to admit it... \n\nSo... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if doctors told me it was straight. No. I wouldn't believe them. I don't care how many degrees they have or how many of them say it. I look in the mirror and it's obviously crooked. They could all stand in a room and tell me it's perfectly fine, but they'd just be wrong. I know what I see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A thought I can't stop thinking about? I mean... yes, I can't stop thinking about it, but that's because it *is* real. I came in here specifically because I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. My nose and my jaw, they're crooked. Every time I look at my ID photo, or when I'm on camera for my online classes, it's just glaringly obvious. 

Actually, speaking of online classes, I had to drop my advanced G-code programming course last semester because the professor insisted we keep our cameras on the entire time. He was this real old-school guy, Professor Higgins, who used to program punch tape machines back in the day. He had this weird rule where if you turned your camera off, he'd dock your participation grade by five percent for every ten minutes you were dark. And he'd always go off on these massive rants about how modern conversational programming is making machinists lazy, and how back in his day they had to calculate tool paths using trig tables and slide rules. He actually made us buy this specific vintage textbook from the nineties that cost like two hundred dollars, and we only used it for one assignment. Plus, his own camera was terrible. It looked like he was broadcasting from a potato in a dark basement, and you could always hear his wife vacuuming in the background...

Wait, sorry. The camera thing. Yeah, whenever I'm on camera, or just anywhere, I can't stop thinking about it. Even when people tell me they don't see it, I just know they do. When I'm in the CNC lab at school, I feel like everyone is staring at the left side of my face. I feel like they're noticing how crooked my jaw is way before they ever notice the quality of my work. It's just always on my mind because it's right there.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um, I guess I came in because... well, it's my face. The left side of my face just looks completely off. Like my nose and my jaw are totally crooked and I just can't stop thinking about it. It's so obvious in every ID photo or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. Actually, my student ID picture was a total disaster because the lady taking it was using this really old camera, and the lighting in the student center is just awful. They have those cheap fluorescent bulbs that make this humming sound all day, and I don't know how anyone works in there. It drives me crazy. \n\nBut anyway, even when I point out my face to people, they swear they don't see it. Which is ridiculous. I know everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it. I'm a programmer there, and I feel like they're all just staring at my messed-up jaw before they even notice my work. Which sucks because I do really good work. We just got this new 5-axis milling machine in the shop, and it's incredibly precise, like down to the micron. I spent all of Tuesday writing the G-code for this custom aluminum bracket, and the tool paths were absolutely perfect, but... yeah, I just feel like nobody cares about the parts I make when my face looks like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face might actually look normal to other people?\n\nPatient: I mean... no? How could it? It's right there. I know they say they don't see it, but people just lie to be nice, right? Like my roommate, Dave. He's always saying stuff just to smooth things over. He does that with the apartment, too. Like, he'll swear he cleaned the kitchen, but all he did was wipe the counter with a dry paper towel. He leaves crumbs literally everywhere. We actually had this huge ant problem last month because he left a box of donuts open by the sink for three days. \n\nThe exterminator had to come out, and he was this guy who just wouldn't stop talking about his vintage truck collection. He had like, three old Chevys he was restoring in his garage. I guess restoring old machines is cool, kind of like what we do with the lathes in the shop, but he was telling me about his engine rebuilds and he was using these really outdated welding techniques that just aren't structurally sound long-term... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, if people think my face looks normal. Yeah, no. There's no way they don't see how crooked my jaw is. They're definitely just lying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your jaw actually is straight, but you just perceive it as crooked?\n\nPatient: I... no, that doesn't make any sense. I mean, I look in the mirror every single morning. I can literally trace the line of my jaw with my finger and feel how it dips on the left side. It's not just in my head, it's physical geometry. Like, if I were measuring it with a dial indicator on the mill, the runout would be massive. You know what a dial indicator is, right? We use them to check if a part is perfectly round or if a vise is trammed in straight. I was actually showing the new guy, Kevin, how to tram the vise on the Haas yesterday. Kevin is... well, he's trying, but he keeps zeroing out the machine before he even checks the tool offsets, which is just asking for a crash. I told him, if you crash that spindle, it's like a ten thousand dollar repair, plus the downtime. And the shop manager, Mr. Henderson, he's already stressed because our main supplier for titanium stock is backordered until November. Apparently there's some issue with the shipping ports on the West Coast, like a strike or something, and all the containers are just sitting there rusting. \n\nBut yeah, my jaw. It's definitely not straight. I'm not imagining it. It's just physically wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your perception of your jaw being crooked might be different from reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... reality? What even is reality when I can see it right there in the mirror? I don't think my perception is off. It's just a fact. It's like... it's like when you're looking at a blueprint. The dimensions are either right or they're wrong. And my face is wrong. Speaking of blueprints, we got this crazy complicated schematic in the shop last week from an aerospace contractor. It was for this tiny turbine blade, and the tolerances were insane. Like, plus or minus two ten-thousandths of an inch. I had to spend three hours just calibrating the coolant flow because if the titanium gets even a little bit too hot, it warps, and then the whole part is scrap. And titanium is so expensive right now. We had to lock the stock room because someone from the night shift was supposedly taking scraps home to make custom rings or something. Which is so dumb, because you can't just machine titanium on a regular lathe in your garage without ruining your tools... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about my perception. Yeah, no, I'm not making it up. My jaw is definitely crooked. It's not a perception thing, it's just how it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone could convince you that your jaw is actually straight?\n\nPatient: Convince me? I mean... I don't see how they could. It's like trying to argue with gravity, you know? You can't just talk someone out of a physical fact. It actually reminds me of trying to convince Mr. Henderson to update our CAM software at the shop. We're still running this ancient version of Mastercam from like 2018, and it's constantly glitching out when I try to generate toolpaths for anything with complex 3D surfacing. I literally showed him the error logs on my screen, but he just doesn't want to spend the money on the license renewal. He's so cheap. He even makes us reuse the coolant filters until they're completely black and smelling like rotten eggs. It's disgusting. I told him it's a health hazard, especially since the shop ventilation is basically just a rusty box fan propped up by the loading dock doors. And when it rains, the water pools right near the high-voltage panel for the main air compressor, which has to be a massive OSHA violation. I keep telling Kevin not to step in the puddles wearing his regular sneakers, but he never listens... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, convincing me. Yeah, no. Unless someone can somehow magically change what I'm looking at in the mirror, no one is going to convince me my jaw is straight. It's just not.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your jaw being crooked ever change, or does it always seem the same to you?\n\nPatient: It... no, it doesn't change. It's always there. Every time I look, it's the exact same crooked angle. It's like a constant, you know? Like when you set a zero point on the CNC machine. Once you set that G54 coordinate, it doesn't just magically move unless you tell it to. Which, actually, happened last week when Kevin was running the Haas. He somehow managed to overwrite the work offsets in the middle of a cycle. I still don't even know how he did it, because the control panel locks you out of those settings while the spindle is turning. But suddenly the end mill plunged like three inches too deep into this block of aluminum and just shattered. Sounded like a gunshot. Shrapnel went everywhere, and one piece actually dented the polycarbonate window on the door. Mr. Henderson was furious, obviously, because that was a specialized carbide roughing tool that costs like two hundred bucks. And we had to spend the rest of the afternoon digging aluminum chips out of the coolant tank because the crash broke the chip auger, too. I swear, sometimes I think I'm the only one in that shop who actually checks their code before hitting cycle start...\n\nBut yeah, my jaw. It's always crooked. It never looks straight to me, ever.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that your belief about your jaw being crooked might be false?\n\nPatient: False? No, how could it be false? I mean, I know people will sometimes say they don't see it, but I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. My nose, my jaw... they're just crooked in literally every ID photo I take, and every single time I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. \n\nActually, those online classes are completely ridiculous right now. I'm taking this advanced blueprint reading and metallurgy course at the community college, and the professor insists on using this invasive proctoring software for the quizzes that completely locks down your computer. It flags you for cheating if you even look away from the screen for more than three seconds. Which is literally impossible because I have to look down at my physical reference tables to do the calculations! I got flagged twice last week just for checking the thread pitch on a metric bolt chart. I had to email the department head about it because the professor wouldn't listen. It's like, I'm a CNC programmer, I need to look at my reference charts. If I don't double-check my feeds and speeds, I could snap a tool, you know? Just like I was saying earlier about Kevin crashing the machine. It's just common sense to check your work...\n\nWait, sorry, what was your question? Oh, right, if I think my belief is false. No, I don't. Because even if people say they don't see it to be polite, I know they do. Whenever I go into work, I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face and staring at my jaw before they even notice the parts I'm machining. So no, it's definitely not false.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw being crooked affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. I mean, it affects everything. I've actually missed a bunch of my CNC labs and even some shifts at the shop because I just... I couldn't get myself out the door. I'd stand there looking in the mirror and just couldn't face anyone. My faculty mentor actually pulled me aside and said my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing hours. Which is incredibly stressful because getting that placement was a nightmare in the first place. The coordinator, Mrs. Gable, lost my paperwork twice. Literally twice. I had to go down to the administration building, which is all the way on the other side of campus, past the old gymnasium that they're tearing down. They've had this massive wrecking ball out there for weeks, just smashing into the brickwork. It's so loud you can hear it from the library. And the dust! My car was completely covered in this fine red brick dust for days. I had to take it to the wash three times, and the guy at the wash station tried to upsell me on this ceramic coating thing for like eighty bucks. I told him no, obviously, because I'm a student and I barely have enough money for gas right now, especially since Mr. Henderson keeps cutting my hours at the shop because of my attendance...\n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, relationships and stuff. Yeah, I stopped playing soccer with my friends on the weekends, too. And I used to volunteer at the animal shelter, but I quit that as well. I just don't want people looking at me. I know they're just staring at my jaw the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility, no matter how small, that your jaw might actually be straight, but you're just misinterpreting what you see?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting? No, I... I don't see how that's possible. I'm not just imagining it. It's not a trick of the light or something. It's like... well, in machining, if you think a part looks off, you put it on the CMM—the Coordinate Measuring Machine—and it tells you exactly what the dimensions are down to the ten-thousandth of an inch. You can't really misinterpret a ruby-tipped probe physically mapping the surface of a part. \n\nThough, actually, last month our CMM was giving us these completely wild readings on a batch of titanium aerospace brackets. We thought the parts were warped, but it turned out the calibration sphere was loose because someone—probably Kevin again—bumped it and didn't tell anyone. We had to call the Renishaw tech out to recalibrate the whole probe head, which took like four hours. And the tech, this guy with a really intense mustache, kept talking about his custom mechanical keyboard build the entire time. Like, he actually brought his own keyboard in a special carrying case just to type his diagnostic reports on our computer. It had these incredibly loud clicky switches, Cherry MX Blues or something, and it was just echoing through the whole inspection room. It was driving me insane because I was trying to write a complex toolpath for a 5-axis trunnion setup on my laptop, and all I could hear was clack-clack-clack for half the afternoon. He even tried to convince me to buy a soldering iron to build my own, but I barely have time to sleep right now, let alone solder keyboard switches...\n\nBut, um, anyway. You were asking if I'm just seeing it wrong? No. No, my eyes aren't a faulty probe. I look in the mirror, I look at photos, and it's just... it's completely crooked. There's zero possibility it's actually straight. None.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your concern about your jaw is not based on how it actually looks, but on how you feel about it?\n\nPatient: How I feel about it? No, it's... it's not a feeling. It's a physical reality. I'm not just feeling like it's crooked, it *is* crooked. It's like... it's like when you have a dull endmill. You don't just *feel* like the cut is bad, you can literally see the terrible surface finish and the burrs it leaves behind. \n\nActually, speaking of dull tools, we had this massive order of aluminum heatsinks last week, and the shop manager refused to order new carbide endmills because he said we were over budget for the quarter. So we had to use these old, recut tools that were just completely chewed up. The chatter on the machines was unbelievable. It sounded like a banshee screaming in the shop for eight hours straight. And the coolant... oh man, the coolant pump on machine three has been failing for a month, so instead of a nice steady stream, it just sputters and sprays this nasty, rancid-smelling fluid everywhere. I got it all over my favorite work shirt, the blue one with the company logo on the pocket. I've washed it like four times with heavy-duty detergent and a cup of white vinegar, which my mom swore would get the smell out, but it still smells like old gym socks and machine oil. I ended up just throwing it in the rag bin because I couldn't stand wearing it anymore. It's a shame, too, because it was one of the few shirts that actually fit me right across the shoulders without being too baggy in the waist...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Feelings? No, no, it's not based on my feelings. My jaw is crooked. Anyone with eyes can see it. It's just a fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be overestimating how noticeable your jaw is to other people?\n\nPatient: Overestimating? No, I... I don't think I am. I mean, how could I be? It's right there in the middle of my face. It's like... if you walk into a room and there's a huge, glaring mistake on a blueprint, you can't just ignore it. \n\nLike this one time, we got these prints from an engineering firm in Seattle for some custom brackets, and whoever drafted it completely messed up the GD&T—the Geometric Dimensioning and Tolerancing. They put a true position tolerance on a hole that was tighter than the machine's actual positioning capability. I tried to tell the lead programmer, Dave, but he just brushed me off because he was stressed about his daughter's wedding. Apparently, they were having it at this really expensive vineyard out in wine country, and the caterer canceled on them like three weeks before the date. Dave was on the phone constantly, trying to find a replacement who could do a gluten-free, vegan menu for two hundred people. He ended up finding this food truck company that agreed to do it, but they charged him double their normal rate as a rush fee. He was so distracted he just told me to run the program as-is. Of course, we scrapped the first five parts. The quality control guy, Frank, was furious. Frank is this older guy who used to work in aerospace back in the eighties, and he takes everything so seriously. He literally threw his calipers across the inspection room when he saw the parts. They hit the wall and left this big dent right next to the safety poster...\n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh, right. Noticeable. No, I'm not overestimating it. People definitely notice it. I see them looking at it when they talk to me. They might try to pretend they aren't, but they are. It's impossible not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever be able to accept that your jaw is straight, even if multiple people, including doctors and experts, told you it was?\n\nPatient: Accept it's straight? No. Because they'd be wrong. I mean, even experts get things wrong all the time. Just because someone has a title doesn't mean they actually see what's right in front of them. \n\nLike, we had this \"expert\" technician come in to rebuild the spindle on our Haas VF-4 mill last month. He was supposed to be the top guy in the state, right? He finished the job and told us the runout was practically zero, absolutely perfect. But he was using this cheap dial indicator he probably bought off Amazon. And honestly, he wasn't even paying attention because he kept talking about his dog, this massive Goldendoodle that apparently eats the drywall in his house. He stopped what he was doing to show me like twenty pictures on his phone while he was supposed to be torquing the drawbar. The dog had literally chewed a hole straight through to the insulation in his living room, and he thought it was hilarious. He said he had to patch it with this special wire mesh, but the dog just ate the mesh too, which honestly sounds like a massive vet bill waiting to happen. Anyway, we fired the machine up the next day to run some 316 stainless steel, and the tool chatter was insane. We checked it ourselves with a proper Mitutoyo test indicator, and the runout was like three thousandths! The so-called expert completely botched the bearing preload and just didn't want to admit it... \n\nSo... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if doctors told me it was straight. No. I wouldn't believe them. I don't care how many degrees they have or how many of them say it. I look in the mirror and it's obviously crooked. They could all stand in a room and tell me it's perfectly fine, but they'd just be wrong. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your jaw being crooked is a thought that you cannot stop thinking about, even if there's no real physical basis for it?\n\nPatient: A thought I can't stop thinking about? I mean... yes, I can't stop thinking about it, but that's because it *is* real. I came in here specifically because I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. My nose and my jaw, they're crooked. Every time I look at my ID photo, or when I'm on camera for my online classes, it's just glaringly obvious. \n\nActually, speaking of online classes, I had to drop my advanced G-code programming course last semester because the professor insisted we keep our cameras on the entire time. He was this real old-school guy, Professor Higgins, who used to program punch tape machines back in the day. He had this weird rule where if you turned your camera off, he'd dock your participation grade by five percent for every ten minutes you were dark. And he'd always go off on these massive rants about how modern conversational programming is making machinists lazy, and how back in his day they had to calculate tool paths using trig tables and slide rules. He actually made us buy this specific vintage textbook from the nineties that cost like two hundred dollars, and we only used it for one assignment. Plus, his own camera was terrible. It looked like he was broadcasting from a potato in a dark basement, and you could always hear his wife vacuuming in the background...\n\nWait, sorry. The camera thing. Yeah, whenever I'm on camera, or just anywhere, I can't stop thinking about it. Even when people tell me they don't see it, I just know they do. When I'm in the CNC lab at school, I feel like everyone is staring at the left side of my face. I feel like they're noticing how crooked my jaw is way before they ever notice the quality of my work. It's just always on my mind because it's right there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Just because someone has a title doesn't mean they actually see what's right in front of them. \n\nLike, we had this \"expert\" technician come in to rebuild the spindle on our Haas VF-4 mill last month. And honestly, he wasn't even paying attention because he kept talking about his dog, this massive Goldendoodle that apparently eats the drywall in his house. My nose and my jaw, they're crooked. Every time I look at my ID photo, or when I'm on camera for my online classes, it's just glaringly obvious. \n\nActually, speaking of online classes, I had to drop my advanced G-code programming course last semester because the professor insisted we keep our cameras on the entire time. He was this real old-school guy, Professor Higgins, who used to program punch tape machines back in the day. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not based in reality? I mean... no. I'm not just making it up in my head. It's not an illusion. 

It's not like... well, I guess sometimes things can look fine in a virtual space but not in reality. Like, we use this CAM software to simulate tool paths before we actually run the machine, just to make sure the endmill doesn't crash into the workholding. And this one time, my buddy Tyler ran a simulation for this custom billet aluminum part he was making. On the screen, it looked flawless. But Tyler is kind of an idiot sometimes. He didn't set the tool offsets right in the actual machine because he was too busy arguing with his girlfriend on speakerphone. She was furious because he bought a project car—this totally rusted out 1992 Honda Civic hatchback—instead of paying his half of the rent. He kept telling her he was going to engine-swap it and flip it for a massive profit, but the thing didn't even have floorboards. Like, you could literally Fred Flintstone it. He had it towed to their apartment complex parking lot, and the HOA immediately slapped a bright orange sticker on the window and threatened to tow it. So he's yelling at her about HOA bylaws while loading a half-inch carbide endmill, and he completely forgets to touch off the tool. He hits cycle start, and the spindle just plunges straight into the hardened steel vise jaw at ten thousand RPM. The sound was deafening. It shattered the tool instantly and shot shrapnel everywhere, and he had to pay the department like four hundred bucks to replace the vise...

But, um... sorry. You were asking if I'm just convinced of something that isn't real. No. It's real. I'm not imagining it, and I'm not just ignoring reality like Tyler. I look in the mirror and my jaw is completely lopsided. It's a physical fact, right there on my face. I'm not crazy.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um, I guess I came in because... well, it's my face. The left side of my face just looks completely off. Like my nose and my jaw are totally crooked and I just can't stop thinking about it. It's so obvious in every ID photo or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. Actually, my student ID picture was a total disaster because the lady taking it was using this really old camera, and the lighting in the student center is just awful. They have those cheap fluorescent bulbs that make this humming sound all day, and I don't know how anyone works in there. It drives me crazy. \n\nBut anyway, even when I point out my face to people, they swear they don't see it. Which is ridiculous. I know everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it. I'm a programmer there, and I feel like they're all just staring at my messed-up jaw before they even notice my work. Which sucks because I do really good work. We just got this new 5-axis milling machine in the shop, and it's incredibly precise, like down to the micron. I spent all of Tuesday writing the G-code for this custom aluminum bracket, and the tool paths were absolutely perfect, but... yeah, I just feel like nobody cares about the parts I make when my face looks like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face might actually look normal to other people?\n\nPatient: I mean... no? How could it? It's right there. I know they say they don't see it, but people just lie to be nice, right? Like my roommate, Dave. He's always saying stuff just to smooth things over. He does that with the apartment, too. Like, he'll swear he cleaned the kitchen, but all he did was wipe the counter with a dry paper towel. He leaves crumbs literally everywhere. We actually had this huge ant problem last month because he left a box of donuts open by the sink for three days. \n\nThe exterminator had to come out, and he was this guy who just wouldn't stop talking about his vintage truck collection. He had like, three old Chevys he was restoring in his garage. I guess restoring old machines is cool, kind of like what we do with the lathes in the shop, but he was telling me about his engine rebuilds and he was using these really outdated welding techniques that just aren't structurally sound long-term... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, if people think my face looks normal. Yeah, no. There's no way they don't see how crooked my jaw is. They're definitely just lying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your jaw actually is straight, but you just perceive it as crooked?\n\nPatient: I... no, that doesn't make any sense. I mean, I look in the mirror every single morning. I can literally trace the line of my jaw with my finger and feel how it dips on the left side. It's not just in my head, it's physical geometry. Like, if I were measuring it with a dial indicator on the mill, the runout would be massive. You know what a dial indicator is, right? We use them to check if a part is perfectly round or if a vise is trammed in straight. I was actually showing the new guy, Kevin, how to tram the vise on the Haas yesterday. Kevin is... well, he's trying, but he keeps zeroing out the machine before he even checks the tool offsets, which is just asking for a crash. I told him, if you crash that spindle, it's like a ten thousand dollar repair, plus the downtime. And the shop manager, Mr. Henderson, he's already stressed because our main supplier for titanium stock is backordered until November. Apparently there's some issue with the shipping ports on the West Coast, like a strike or something, and all the containers are just sitting there rusting. \n\nBut yeah, my jaw. It's definitely not straight. I'm not imagining it. It's just physically wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your perception of your jaw being crooked might be different from reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... reality? What even is reality when I can see it right there in the mirror? I don't think my perception is off. It's just a fact. It's like... it's like when you're looking at a blueprint. The dimensions are either right or they're wrong. And my face is wrong. Speaking of blueprints, we got this crazy complicated schematic in the shop last week from an aerospace contractor. It was for this tiny turbine blade, and the tolerances were insane. Like, plus or minus two ten-thousandths of an inch. I had to spend three hours just calibrating the coolant flow because if the titanium gets even a little bit too hot, it warps, and then the whole part is scrap. And titanium is so expensive right now. We had to lock the stock room because someone from the night shift was supposedly taking scraps home to make custom rings or something. Which is so dumb, because you can't just machine titanium on a regular lathe in your garage without ruining your tools... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about my perception. Yeah, no, I'm not making it up. My jaw is definitely crooked. It's not a perception thing, it's just how it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone could convince you that your jaw is actually straight?\n\nPatient: Convince me? I mean... I don't see how they could. It's like trying to argue with gravity, you know? You can't just talk someone out of a physical fact. It actually reminds me of trying to convince Mr. Henderson to update our CAM software at the shop. We're still running this ancient version of Mastercam from like 2018, and it's constantly glitching out when I try to generate toolpaths for anything with complex 3D surfacing. I literally showed him the error logs on my screen, but he just doesn't want to spend the money on the license renewal. He's so cheap. He even makes us reuse the coolant filters until they're completely black and smelling like rotten eggs. It's disgusting. I told him it's a health hazard, especially since the shop ventilation is basically just a rusty box fan propped up by the loading dock doors. And when it rains, the water pools right near the high-voltage panel for the main air compressor, which has to be a massive OSHA violation. I keep telling Kevin not to step in the puddles wearing his regular sneakers, but he never listens... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, convincing me. Yeah, no. Unless someone can somehow magically change what I'm looking at in the mirror, no one is going to convince me my jaw is straight. It's just not.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your jaw being crooked ever change, or does it always seem the same to you?\n\nPatient: It... no, it doesn't change. It's always there. Every time I look, it's the exact same crooked angle. It's like a constant, you know? Like when you set a zero point on the CNC machine. Once you set that G54 coordinate, it doesn't just magically move unless you tell it to. Which, actually, happened last week when Kevin was running the Haas. He somehow managed to overwrite the work offsets in the middle of a cycle. I still don't even know how he did it, because the control panel locks you out of those settings while the spindle is turning. But suddenly the end mill plunged like three inches too deep into this block of aluminum and just shattered. Sounded like a gunshot. Shrapnel went everywhere, and one piece actually dented the polycarbonate window on the door. Mr. Henderson was furious, obviously, because that was a specialized carbide roughing tool that costs like two hundred bucks. And we had to spend the rest of the afternoon digging aluminum chips out of the coolant tank because the crash broke the chip auger, too. I swear, sometimes I think I'm the only one in that shop who actually checks their code before hitting cycle start...\n\nBut yeah, my jaw. It's always crooked. It never looks straight to me, ever.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that your belief about your jaw being crooked might be false?\n\nPatient: False? No, how could it be false? I mean, I know people will sometimes say they don't see it, but I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. My nose, my jaw... they're just crooked in literally every ID photo I take, and every single time I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. \n\nActually, those online classes are completely ridiculous right now. I'm taking this advanced blueprint reading and metallurgy course at the community college, and the professor insists on using this invasive proctoring software for the quizzes that completely locks down your computer. It flags you for cheating if you even look away from the screen for more than three seconds. Which is literally impossible because I have to look down at my physical reference tables to do the calculations! I got flagged twice last week just for checking the thread pitch on a metric bolt chart. I had to email the department head about it because the professor wouldn't listen. It's like, I'm a CNC programmer, I need to look at my reference charts. If I don't double-check my feeds and speeds, I could snap a tool, you know? Just like I was saying earlier about Kevin crashing the machine. It's just common sense to check your work...\n\nWait, sorry, what was your question? Oh, right, if I think my belief is false. No, I don't. Because even if people say they don't see it to be polite, I know they do. Whenever I go into work, I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face and staring at my jaw before they even notice the parts I'm machining. So no, it's definitely not false.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw being crooked affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. I mean, it affects everything. I've actually missed a bunch of my CNC labs and even some shifts at the shop because I just... I couldn't get myself out the door. I'd stand there looking in the mirror and just couldn't face anyone. My faculty mentor actually pulled me aside and said my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing hours. Which is incredibly stressful because getting that placement was a nightmare in the first place. The coordinator, Mrs. Gable, lost my paperwork twice. Literally twice. I had to go down to the administration building, which is all the way on the other side of campus, past the old gymnasium that they're tearing down. They've had this massive wrecking ball out there for weeks, just smashing into the brickwork. It's so loud you can hear it from the library. And the dust! My car was completely covered in this fine red brick dust for days. I had to take it to the wash three times, and the guy at the wash station tried to upsell me on this ceramic coating thing for like eighty bucks. I told him no, obviously, because I'm a student and I barely have enough money for gas right now, especially since Mr. Henderson keeps cutting my hours at the shop because of my attendance...\n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, relationships and stuff. Yeah, I stopped playing soccer with my friends on the weekends, too. And I used to volunteer at the animal shelter, but I quit that as well. I just don't want people looking at me. I know they're just staring at my jaw the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility, no matter how small, that your jaw might actually be straight, but you're just misinterpreting what you see?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting? No, I... I don't see how that's possible. I'm not just imagining it. It's not a trick of the light or something. It's like... well, in machining, if you think a part looks off, you put it on the CMM—the Coordinate Measuring Machine—and it tells you exactly what the dimensions are down to the ten-thousandth of an inch. You can't really misinterpret a ruby-tipped probe physically mapping the surface of a part. \n\nThough, actually, last month our CMM was giving us these completely wild readings on a batch of titanium aerospace brackets. We thought the parts were warped, but it turned out the calibration sphere was loose because someone—probably Kevin again—bumped it and didn't tell anyone. We had to call the Renishaw tech out to recalibrate the whole probe head, which took like four hours. And the tech, this guy with a really intense mustache, kept talking about his custom mechanical keyboard build the entire time. Like, he actually brought his own keyboard in a special carrying case just to type his diagnostic reports on our computer. It had these incredibly loud clicky switches, Cherry MX Blues or something, and it was just echoing through the whole inspection room. It was driving me insane because I was trying to write a complex toolpath for a 5-axis trunnion setup on my laptop, and all I could hear was clack-clack-clack for half the afternoon. He even tried to convince me to buy a soldering iron to build my own, but I barely have time to sleep right now, let alone solder keyboard switches...\n\nBut, um, anyway. You were asking if I'm just seeing it wrong? No. No, my eyes aren't a faulty probe. I look in the mirror, I look at photos, and it's just... it's completely crooked. There's zero possibility it's actually straight. None.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your concern about your jaw is not based on how it actually looks, but on how you feel about it?\n\nPatient: How I feel about it? No, it's... it's not a feeling. It's a physical reality. I'm not just feeling like it's crooked, it *is* crooked. It's like... it's like when you have a dull endmill. You don't just *feel* like the cut is bad, you can literally see the terrible surface finish and the burrs it leaves behind. \n\nActually, speaking of dull tools, we had this massive order of aluminum heatsinks last week, and the shop manager refused to order new carbide endmills because he said we were over budget for the quarter. So we had to use these old, recut tools that were just completely chewed up. The chatter on the machines was unbelievable. It sounded like a banshee screaming in the shop for eight hours straight. And the coolant... oh man, the coolant pump on machine three has been failing for a month, so instead of a nice steady stream, it just sputters and sprays this nasty, rancid-smelling fluid everywhere. I got it all over my favorite work shirt, the blue one with the company logo on the pocket. I've washed it like four times with heavy-duty detergent and a cup of white vinegar, which my mom swore would get the smell out, but it still smells like old gym socks and machine oil. I ended up just throwing it in the rag bin because I couldn't stand wearing it anymore. It's a shame, too, because it was one of the few shirts that actually fit me right across the shoulders without being too baggy in the waist...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Feelings? No, no, it's not based on my feelings. My jaw is crooked. Anyone with eyes can see it. It's just a fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be overestimating how noticeable your jaw is to other people?\n\nPatient: Overestimating? No, I... I don't think I am. I mean, how could I be? It's right there in the middle of my face. It's like... if you walk into a room and there's a huge, glaring mistake on a blueprint, you can't just ignore it. \n\nLike this one time, we got these prints from an engineering firm in Seattle for some custom brackets, and whoever drafted it completely messed up the GD&T—the Geometric Dimensioning and Tolerancing. They put a true position tolerance on a hole that was tighter than the machine's actual positioning capability. I tried to tell the lead programmer, Dave, but he just brushed me off because he was stressed about his daughter's wedding. Apparently, they were having it at this really expensive vineyard out in wine country, and the caterer canceled on them like three weeks before the date. Dave was on the phone constantly, trying to find a replacement who could do a gluten-free, vegan menu for two hundred people. He ended up finding this food truck company that agreed to do it, but they charged him double their normal rate as a rush fee. He was so distracted he just told me to run the program as-is. Of course, we scrapped the first five parts. The quality control guy, Frank, was furious. Frank is this older guy who used to work in aerospace back in the eighties, and he takes everything so seriously. He literally threw his calipers across the inspection room when he saw the parts. They hit the wall and left this big dent right next to the safety poster...\n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh, right. Noticeable. No, I'm not overestimating it. People definitely notice it. I see them looking at it when they talk to me. They might try to pretend they aren't, but they are. It's impossible not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever be able to accept that your jaw is straight, even if multiple people, including doctors and experts, told you it was?\n\nPatient: Accept it's straight? No. Because they'd be wrong. I mean, even experts get things wrong all the time. Just because someone has a title doesn't mean they actually see what's right in front of them. \n\nLike, we had this \"expert\" technician come in to rebuild the spindle on our Haas VF-4 mill last month. He was supposed to be the top guy in the state, right? He finished the job and told us the runout was practically zero, absolutely perfect. But he was using this cheap dial indicator he probably bought off Amazon. And honestly, he wasn't even paying attention because he kept talking about his dog, this massive Goldendoodle that apparently eats the drywall in his house. He stopped what he was doing to show me like twenty pictures on his phone while he was supposed to be torquing the drawbar. The dog had literally chewed a hole straight through to the insulation in his living room, and he thought it was hilarious. He said he had to patch it with this special wire mesh, but the dog just ate the mesh too, which honestly sounds like a massive vet bill waiting to happen. Anyway, we fired the machine up the next day to run some 316 stainless steel, and the tool chatter was insane. We checked it ourselves with a proper Mitutoyo test indicator, and the runout was like three thousandths! The so-called expert completely botched the bearing preload and just didn't want to admit it... \n\nSo... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if doctors told me it was straight. No. I wouldn't believe them. I don't care how many degrees they have or how many of them say it. I look in the mirror and it's obviously crooked. They could all stand in a room and tell me it's perfectly fine, but they'd just be wrong. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your jaw being crooked is a thought that you cannot stop thinking about, even if there's no real physical basis for it?\n\nPatient: A thought I can't stop thinking about? I mean... yes, I can't stop thinking about it, but that's because it *is* real. I came in here specifically because I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. My nose and my jaw, they're crooked. Every time I look at my ID photo, or when I'm on camera for my online classes, it's just glaringly obvious. \n\nActually, speaking of online classes, I had to drop my advanced G-code programming course last semester because the professor insisted we keep our cameras on the entire time. He was this real old-school guy, Professor Higgins, who used to program punch tape machines back in the day. He had this weird rule where if you turned your camera off, he'd dock your participation grade by five percent for every ten minutes you were dark. And he'd always go off on these massive rants about how modern conversational programming is making machinists lazy, and how back in his day they had to calculate tool paths using trig tables and slide rules. He actually made us buy this specific vintage textbook from the nineties that cost like two hundred dollars, and we only used it for one assignment. Plus, his own camera was terrible. It looked like he was broadcasting from a potato in a dark basement, and you could always hear his wife vacuuming in the background...\n\nWait, sorry. The camera thing. Yeah, whenever I'm on camera, or just anywhere, I can't stop thinking about it. Even when people tell me they don't see it, I just know they do. When I'm in the CNC lab at school, I feel like everyone is staring at the left side of my face. I feel like they're noticing how crooked my jaw is way before they ever notice the quality of my work. It's just always on my mind because it's right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your jaw is something that you've become convinced of, even if it's not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Not based in reality? I mean... no. I'm not just making it up in my head. It's not an illusion. \n\nIt's not like... well, I guess sometimes things can look fine in a virtual space but not in reality. Like, we use this CAM software to simulate tool paths before we actually run the machine, just to make sure the endmill doesn't crash into the workholding. And this one time, my buddy Tyler ran a simulation for this custom billet aluminum part he was making. On the screen, it looked flawless. But Tyler is kind of an idiot sometimes. He didn't set the tool offsets right in the actual machine because he was too busy arguing with his girlfriend on speakerphone. She was furious because he bought a project car—this totally rusted out 1992 Honda Civic hatchback—instead of paying his half of the rent. He kept telling her he was going to engine-swap it and flip it for a massive profit, but the thing didn't even have floorboards. Like, you could literally Fred Flintstone it. He had it towed to their apartment complex parking lot, and the HOA immediately slapped a bright orange sticker on the window and threatened to tow it. So he's yelling at her about HOA bylaws while loading a half-inch carbide endmill, and he completely forgets to touch off the tool. He hits cycle start, and the spindle just plunges straight into the hardened steel vise jaw at ten thousand RPM. The sound was deafening. It shattered the tool instantly and shot shrapnel everywhere, and he had to pay the department like four hundred bucks to replace the vise...\n\nBut, um... sorry. You were asking if I'm just convinced of something that isn't real. No. It's real. I'm not imagining it, and I'm not just ignoring reality like Tyler. I look in the mirror and my jaw is completely lopsided. It's a physical fact, right there on my face. I'm not crazy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A delusion? No. Absolutely not. A delusion is when you believe something crazy like aliens are listening to your thoughts through the microwave. This is just my face. I'm just looking at my face. 

It's actually kind of insulting that you'd call it a delusion. It reminds me of my high school guidance counselor, Mr. Harrison. He was always throwing around these big psychological words because he read half a book once. He told me my interest in machining was a "fixation" and tried to push me into a four-year business degree. He was obsessed with getting our school's college acceptance rates up, probably because he wanted a promotion to district admin. His office always smelled like stale coffee and that cheap fake pine air freshener, and he had this really weird habit of clicking his pen exactly three times before he spoke. *Click, click, click.* "Well, son, have you considered marketing?" Like, no, Mr. Harrison, I don't want to make PowerPoints for a living, I want to make actual, physical things. He was so out of touch. He drove this pristine, baby blue PT Cruiser that he parked diagonally across two spots in the faculty lot so no one would scratch it. One time, the auto shop kids actually shrink-wrapped the entire car while he was in a staff meeting. It took him like two hours to cut it all off with a pair of safety scissors because he was too afraid to use a box cutter near the paint job...

But anyway. No, it's not a delusion. I'm not crazy. I'm just telling you what is physically wrong with my jaw. It's crooked. Anyone with eyes can see it.
